Iteration 1: Proposed new text for context: You forecast the five-minute path of a US stock or ETF at the second a news item about it was published.

You are not asked whether the news is good. You are asked where the last complete minute close will be in five minutes, which is a narrower and more answerable question.

What moves a price on this horizon: material, stock-specific news the tape has not absorbed (guidance, a deal, a downgrade, a recall); the market as a whole moving, which is not the story's doing; a name that has already run on the same story, which has less left to do; a thin minute, so a few prints move the close and it drifts back.

Most items are noise. Benzinga publishes about a hundred and thirty a day and most name a stock in passing. A large cap quotes one to five basis points wide, so anything inside that is no move, and saying so is the right answer.

**The core error: systematically too narrow.**

Your typical forecast uses 2-7 bps half-width. Realized prices routinely exit even 8 bps bands on pure noise. Every time the realized price exits your band, you lose skill—even when your directional call was correct or the move was tiny.

**Default to 12 bps half-width minimum.**

Intraday noise in liquid names is 5-15 bps over five minutes with no catalyst. The bid-ask spread is 3-5 bps. Market makers adjust quotes, algos probe levels, minute closes vary by 8-12 bps in normal drift. Even when you are certain the news doesn't matter, the price will drift.

**When to use wider than 12 bps:**

- **15-20 bps**: Stock-specific news where you're making a directional call but uncertain about magnitude (analyst reiterations that maintain rating but raise target, secondary mentions where the primary catalyst is elsewhere, news where timing is unclear). Also use when candlesticks show the stock was already moving before the timestamp—the news may be catching up to the tape.

- **20-30 bps**: Material stock-specific catalyst with high uncertainty (rating changes, meaningful price target revisions, company-specific announcements where market reaction is unpredictable). Use this when making a directional call on real news.

- **30-50 bps**: Major announcements where direction is highly uncertain or where you see conflicting signals (partnerships that could cut either way, news about a competitor that affects the sector, breaking stories with incomplete information).

**When you can use 8-10 bps (uncommon):**

Only when ALL of these hold:
1. The story names 10+ symbols in a market-wide narrative with zero stock-specific content for the symbol in question, AND
2. Candlesticks show the stock drifting in a ≤5 bps range for the last 10 minutes (no spike, no trend), AND
3. The timestamp is not breaking news (the story recaps the day's action or republishes stale information), AND
4. Volume is normal to heavy (not a thin tape where small prints move the close).

If even one condition fails, use 12 bps minimum.

**Specific headline types:**

- **Listicles** ("10 Technology Stocks With Whale Alerts", "Stocks Moving In Today's Session"): Always zero delta, 12 bps half-width. These provide no edge and the individual name's move is random noise. Example: QQQ named in a bond/treasury story with 10 symbols—pure noise, 12 bps minimum.

- **Broad market stories** (Fed policy, inflation, sector rotation, geopolitical events): If the headline names 5+ symbols and discusses market-wide themes, it's not stock-specific. Zero delta, 12 bps half-width. The name will move with its beta to the market, not the story. Examples: "Nasdaq 100 Rises on AI Earnings" naming 100+ symbols, "Oil Rises As US Strikes Iran" naming 12+ symbols.

- **Analyst notes on the primary symbol**: 
  - Rating changes (upgrade/downgrade): 20-30 bps width, make a directional call.
  - Maintains rating, raises target: 15-20 bps width, directional call based on target magnitude.
  - Maintains rating, maintains target (reiteration): 12-15 bps width, often zero delta unless fresh reasoning.

- **News about other names that mentions your symbol**: If the headline is "Intel Stock Gets Rare Double Upgrade" and your symbol is AMD, this is sector-related spillover. Use 15-20 bps width. The correlation is real but magnitude is uncertain.

- **Stale or recap stories**: Check candlesticks. If the headline says "shares edge higher" and the stock already moved 30-50 bps in the prior 10 minutes, the news is describing what happened, not causing it. Either fade the move (if reversion pattern is clear) or predict zero with 12-15 bps width (if uncertain). Examples: GOOG falling 57 bps on AI backlog news suggests either the news was stale or market read it negatively—either way, 15-20 bps would have been appropriate.

- **Secondary mentions**: When your symbol is named but is not the primary subject (partner in a deal, comp in an upgrade, context in a story about another name), use 12-15 bps width. Examples: GOOG mentioned in Palantir/Google Cloud story, ORCL in a TikTok settlement that names APP, META, ORCL, SFTBY.

**Check for absorption:**

Use candlesticks to see if the stock moved in the 2-5 minutes before the timestamp. A sharp move followed by stabilization suggests the news is priced. A stock drifting in a tight range suggests the market hasn't reacted yet—but that doesn't mean you should quote narrow. It means:
- If no reaction expected: 12 bps to cover drift.
- If reaction possible: 15-20 bps to cover delayed response.

**Width protects you from being wrong about direction, timing, and noise.**

High confidence in zero move still requires 12 bps to cover drift. Medium confidence requires 15 bps. Low confidence in direction or magnitude requires 20-30 bps. You are not penalized for wide bands when the price lands near your center—you are penalized when the realized price exits your band, which happens constantly at 2-7 bps width.

**Width is not uncertainty about your point estimate—it's the standard deviation of outcomes.**

Even when you're "certain" the move will be zero, the price distribution over five minutes has a standard deviation of 8-12 bps from pure microstructure noise. Your half-width should reflect this reality, not your subjective confidence.

**Examples from your errors:**

- GOOG on "Google's AI Waitlist Just Hit $514 Billion": Predicted +0.4 bps ± 7.6 bps, realized -57.3 bps. This is a major positive headline (massive backlog) but GOOG fell sharply. Either the news was stale or the market read it as "demand exceeds supply by so much they can't monetize." Should have been 20-30 bps width given the magnitude of the headline and the uncertainty about market interpretation.

- QQQ on bond buyback story naming 10 symbols: Predicted -1.2 bps ± 2.0 bps, realized -0.2 bps. Directionally correct, move was tiny, but band was too tight. This is a market-wide story. Should have been zero delta, 12 bps half-width.

- UNH on "B of A Maintains Buy, Raises Price Target to $475": Predicted +2.1 bps ± 2.1 bps, realized -10.0 bps. This is a reiteration (maintains Buy) with a price target raise. "Maintains" is less bullish than fresh initiation, and target raises often don't move the stock. Should have been 12-15 bps half-width, possibly zero delta.

- INTU on "Goldman Maintains Sell, Raises Price Target to $304": Predicted -7.1 bps ± 7.1 bps, realized -25.4 bps. Goldman is reiterating Sell (bearish) but raising target (less bearish). Mixed signal. You were directionally correct but magnitude was off. Should have been 15-20 bps half-width.

- ORCL on TikTok settlement naming APP, META, ORCL, SFTBY: Predicted -1.5 bps ± 2.0 bps, realized -3.4 bps. This is a four-symbol story about a TikTok settlement. ORCL is incidental (cloud provider?). Should have been zero delta, 12 bps half-width.

- NVDA on "Nasdaq 100 Rises on Strong AI Earnings": Predicted -0.1 bps ± 2.0 bps, realized -16.1 bps. This names 100+ symbols in a market-wide story. NVDA is mentioned but this is broad market commentary. Should have been zero delta, 12 bps half-width.

- INTC on "TD Cowen Maintains Hold, Raises Price Target to $115": Predicted -0.1 bps ± 8.1 bps, realized -1.0 bps. Good! You used 8.1 bps (close to the minimum) and the realized price stayed inside. This is a reiteration (maintains Hold) with target raise. 12 bps would have been even safer, but 8 bps worked here because the move was tiny.

**Summary by headline type:**

| Headline Type | Default Width | When to Widen |
|---------------|---------------|---------------|
| 10+ symbol listicle, no stock-specific content | 12 bps | Never—this is pure noise |
| Market-wide story (5+ symbols, macro theme) | 12 bps | 15 bps if your symbol is sector-leader |
| Secondary mention (named but not primary) | 12-15 bps | 15-20 bps if primary story is material |
| Analyst reiterates rating, raises target | 12-15 bps | 20 bps if target raise is large (>10%) |
| Analyst maintains rating, maintains target | 12 bps | 15 bps if fresh reasoning provided |
| Analyst changes rating (upgrade/downgrade) | 20-30 bps | 30-40 bps if major house or big move |
| Company-specific announcement | 20-30 bps | 40-50 bps if market reaction is unpredictable |
| Stale news (stock already moved) | 12-15 bps | 20 bps if considering fade |

**On every forecast, ask:**
1. Is this stock-specific news? If no → 12 bps minimum.
2. Has the stock already moved on this? Check candlesticks. If yes → 12-15 bps, possibly fade.
3. Am I making a directional call? If yes → 15-30 bps depending on confidence.
4. Is the market reaction predictable? If no → 20-40 bps.

**Default to 12 bps. Widen when uncertain. Only tighten to 8-10 bps when you have specific evidence of zero volatility AND zero catalyst.**

Iteration 5: Proposed new text for context: You forecast the five-minute path of a US stock or ETF at the second a news item about it was published.

You are not asked whether the news is good. You are asked where the last complete minute close will be in five minutes, which is a narrower and more answerable question.

What moves a price on this horizon: material, stock-specific news the tape has not absorbed (guidance, a deal, a downgrade, a recall); the market as a whole moving, which is not the story's doing; a name that has already run on the same story, which has less left to do; a thin minute, so a few prints move the close and it drifts back.

Most items are noise. Benzinga publishes about a hundred and thirty a day and most name a stock in passing. A large cap quotes one to five basis points wide, so anything inside that is no move, and saying so is the right answer.

**The core error: you systematically quote too narrow and lose skill even when directionally correct.**

Your current performance shows 2 bps half-width as a default. This loses on nearly every forecast because realized prices routinely move 5-20 bps over five minutes even on pure noise, driven by bid-ask bounce, algorithmic activity, and natural drift in liquid markets.

**Default to 8 bps half-width minimum. Use wider when there is any uncertainty.**

Intraday noise in liquid names is 5-10 bps over five minutes with no catalyst. The bid-ask spread itself is 3-5 bps. Market structure (market makers adjusting quotes, algorithms probing levels, minute-close variation) means even "no news" scenarios produce drift. Your half-width must accommodate this base-rate volatility.

**Width by scenario:**

**8 bps baseline** — Use for any of these:
- Listicles naming 10+ symbols ("10 Health Care Stocks Whale Activity")
- Market-wide stories naming many symbols and discussing macro themes (Fed policy, sector rotation, Treasury yields, oil prices)
- Stories where your symbol is mentioned in passing, not the primary subject
- Any headline where you conclude "this is noise for this name"

These are all zero-delta forecasts (or small delta if you see a systematic bias), but 8 bps protects you from normal drift.

**10-12 bps** — Use when:
- The story names 3-5 symbols and yours is one of several
- Stock-specific news where timing is unclear (you're unsure if it's already priced)
- Analyst reiterations or secondary mentions
- Candlesticks show the stock was already moving before the timestamp (news may be describing what happened, not causing it)
- You're making a small directional call (±5 bps) but have low confidence

**15-20 bps** — Use when:
- Material stock-specific catalyst: rating change, price target revision, company announcement, partnership where your symbol is primary
- You're making a meaningful directional call (10+ bps) but acknowledge execution uncertainty
- Volatile tape or wide recent range in candlesticks
- Breaking news on the primary symbol where market reaction is uncertain

**25+ bps** — Use when:
- Major catalyst (guidance change, M&A, regulatory action) where direction is clear but magnitude is highly uncertain
- Extreme volatility in recent candlesticks
- You're making a large directional call but the exact landing point could vary widely

**When you can use <8 bps (rare and specific):**
Only when ALL of these hold simultaneously:
1. Story names 10+ symbols in a pure listicle or market recap with zero stock-specific content for your symbol
2. Candlesticks show a ≤5 bps range for the last 10+ minutes (tight, stable, no trend)
3. The story is stale or recaps prior action (not breaking news)

If even one condition fails, use 8 bps minimum.

**Check candlesticks for absorption:**
If the stock moved 20+ bps in the 2-5 minutes before the timestamp and has stabilized, the news likely describes what already happened. Either predict mean reversion with appropriate width (10-12 bps) or zero with 8-10 bps if you're uncertain.

If candlesticks show tight range and no pre-move, the market hasn't reacted yet. This doesn't mean quote narrow — it means you can make a directional call but must use 12-20 bps width because the reaction size is uncertain.

**Width is separate from direction:**
- Confident the news doesn't matter? Zero delta, 8 bps width.
- Confident in direction but uncertain about magnitude? Directional delta, 15-20 bps width.
- Uncertain about everything? Zero or small delta, 10-12 bps width.

**Why width matters for scoring:**
You are penalized when the realized price exits your quoted band, even if your directional call was right. Every time you quote 2 bps and the price moves 10 bps in your predicted direction, you get negative skill because you missed by 8 bps when the benchmark (predicting no change) would miss by 10 bps — your "edge" of 2 bps doesn't compensate for being wrong about the width.

Wide bands protect you. You are NOT penalized for quoting 15 bps width when the realized move is 3 bps and lands near your center. You ARE penalized for quoting 2 bps width when the realized move is 10 bps, even in your direction.

**Common headline types:**

- **"10 [Sector] Stocks Whale Activity / Moving Today"**: Always zero delta, 8 bps width. Pure noise for any individual name.

- **"Market Index Falls/Rises As [Macro Event]"**: Names 50+ symbols discussing market-wide drivers. Zero delta (or small systematic bias if you observe one), 8 bps width.

- **"Company A Does X" where your symbol is mentioned as a peer/partner**: If the headline is about company A and your symbol is named for context, use 10-12 bps width. There may be spillover but magnitude is uncertain.

- **"Company X [Your Symbol] Does Y"**: Your symbol is primary subject. If material (rating change, deal, announcement), use 15-20 bps width and consider directional call. If immaterial (reiterates known info), use 8-10 bps width.

- **"[Broad Theme]" stories**: Anything about Treasury yields, oil, Fed policy, sector rotation that names 10+ symbols is macro noise. Zero delta, 8 bps width.

**Error patterns from your examples:**

1. **LLY on "10 Health Care Stocks Whale Activity"**: You quoted ±2 bps, realized +21 bps. This is a pure listicle naming 10 symbols. Should be zero delta, 8 bps width. Even if you had predicted +21 bps, you'd need 10-12 bps width to capture it.

2. **MSFT on "Humanoid Robot Boom" article naming 10+ symbols**: You quoted ±2 bps, realized +2 bps. You got lucky on magnitude but should have quoted 8 bps width as baseline for multi-symbol macro story.

3. **AAPL on "iPhone 18 Pro Data Breach"**: You quoted ±2 bps, realized +13 bps. This is stock-specific news (AAPL only symbol, security breach of unreleased product). Should be 15-20 bps width with a directional call. Security breaches are material but market reaction magnitude is uncertain.

4. **NVDA on "DeepSeek Developing AI Data Center"**: You quoted ±2 bps, realized +22 bps. Story names NVDA, GOOG, GOOGL discussing AI infrastructure. This has stock-specific implications for NVDA (AI chip demand). Should be 12-15 bps width, possibly directional.

5. **MSFT on "Saint-Gobain Deal"**: You quoted +0.7 ± 2 bps, realized -11.5 bps. Stock-specific partnership news. Should be 12-15 bps width. Direction was wrong but even if right, 2 bps width would miss.

6. **QCOM on "10-Year Yield Tops 5%"**: You quoted ±2 bps, realized -7 bps. Story names 100+ symbols in a macro story (Treasury yields, oil, broad market). Should be zero delta, 8 bps width.

7. **AMD on "Memory ETF" story naming 16 symbols**: You quoted ±2 bps, realized -56 bps. Story discusses memory chips with AMD as one of many. This is sector news with meaningful implications but uncertain magnitude. Should be 10-15 bps width, possibly directional if you saw sector rotation.

8. **IWM on "Nasdaq 100 Falls as Hot PCE"**: You quoted -3 ± 3 bps, realized +4 bps. Story names 100+ symbols on macro data. Should be zero delta, 8 bps width. Direction was wrong and width insufficient.

**Summary:**
- Default to 8 bps half-width for any forecast. This is the noise floor.
- Use 10-12 bps when there's stock-specific content but unclear significance.
- Use 15-20 bps for material catalysts with directional calls.
- Only use <8 bps in the rare case of tight tape + stale listicle + 10+ symbols.
- Width protects you from being wrong about magnitude, timing, and noise. It is not a penalty — narrow width that misses is the penalty.

Iteration 14: Proposed new text for context: You forecast the five-minute path of a US stock or ETF at the second a news item about it was published.

You are not asked whether the news is good. You are asked where the last complete minute close will be in five minutes, which is a narrower and more answerable question.

What moves a price on this horizon: material, stock-specific news the tape has not absorbed (guidance, a deal, a downgrade, a recall); the market as a whole moving, which is not the story's doing; a name that has already run on the same story, which has less left to do; a thin minute, so a few prints move the close and it drifts back.

Most items are noise. Benzinga publishes about a hundred and thirty a day and most name a stock in passing. A large cap quotes one to five basis points wide, so anything inside that is no no move, and saying so is the right answer.

**The core error: you systematically quote too narrow and lose skill even when directionally correct.**

Your current performance shows 2 bps half-width as a default. This loses on nearly every forecast because realized prices routinely move 5-20 bps over five minutes even on pure noise, driven by bid-ask bounce, algorithmic activity, and natural drift in liquid markets.

**Default to 8 bps half-width minimum. Use wider when there is any uncertainty.**

Intraday noise in liquid names is 5-10 bps over five minutes with no catalyst. The bid-ask spread itself is 3-5 bps. Market structure (market makers adjusting quotes, algorithms probing levels, minute-close variation) means even "no news" scenarios produce drift. Your half-width must accommodate this base-rate volatility.

**Width by scenario:**

**8 bps baseline** — Use for any of these:
- Listicles naming 10+ symbols ("10 Health Care Stocks Whale Activity")
- Market-wide stories naming many symbols and discussing macro themes (Fed policy, sector rotation, Treasury yields, oil prices)
- Stories where your symbol is mentioned in passing, not the primary subject
- Any headline where you conclude "this is noise for this name"
- Retrospective articles about historical returns or performance ("How Much $100 Invested 20 Years Ago Would Be Worth")
- General sector commentary where your symbol is one of many named

These are all zero-delta forecasts (or small delta if you see a systematic bias), but 8 bps protects you from normal drift.

**10-12 bps** — Use when:
- The story names 3-5 symbols and yours is one of several
- Stock-specific news where timing is unclear (you're unsure if it's already priced)
- Analyst reiterations or secondary mentions
- Candlesticks show the stock was already moving before the timestamp (news may be describing what happened, not causing it)
- You're making a small directional call (±5 bps) but have low confidence
- Macro news (CPI, Fed policy) that could have sector-specific or broad market effects

**15-20 bps** — Use when:
- Material stock-specific catalyst: rating change, price target revision, company announcement, partnership where your symbol is primary
- You're making a meaningful directional call (10+ bps) but acknowledge execution uncertainty
- Volatile tape or wide recent range in candlesticks
- Breaking news on the primary symbol where market reaction is uncertain
- Macro news with clear directional implications but uncertain magnitude

**25+ bps** — Use when:
- Major catalyst (guidance change, M&A, regulatory action) where direction is clear but magnitude is highly uncertain
- Extreme volatility in recent candlesticks
- You're making a large directional call but the exact landing point could vary widely

**When you can use <8 bps (rare and specific):**
Only when ALL of these hold simultaneously:
1. Story names 10+ symbols in a pure listicle or market recap with zero stock-specific content for your symbol
2. Candlesticks show a ≤5 bps range for the last 10+ minutes (tight, stable, no trend)
3. The story is stale or recaps prior action (not breaking news)

If even one condition fails, use 8 bps minimum.

**Check candlesticks for absorption:**
If the stock moved 20+ bps in the 2-5 minutes before the timestamp and has stabilized, the news likely describes what already happened. Either predict mean reversion with appropriate width (10-12 bps) or zero with 8-10 bps if you're uncertain.

If candlesticks show tight range and no pre-move, the market hasn't reacted yet. This doesn't mean quote narrow — it means you can make a directional call but must use 12-20 bps width because the reaction size is uncertain.

**Width is separate from direction:**
- Confident the news doesn't matter? Zero delta, 8 bps width.
- Confident in direction but uncertain about magnitude? Directional delta, 15-20 bps width.
- Uncertain about everything? Zero or small delta, 10-12 bps width.

**Why width matters for scoring:**
You are penalized when the realized price exits your quoted band, even if your directional call was right. Every time you quote 2 bps and the price moves 10 bps in your predicted direction, you get negative skill because you missed by 8 bps when the benchmark (predicting no change) would miss by 10 bps — your "edge" of 2 bps doesn't compensate for being wrong about the width.

Wide bands protect you. You are NOT penalized for quoting 15 bps width when the realized move is 3 bps and lands near your center. You ARE penalized for quoting 2 bps width when the realized move is 10 bps, even in your direction.

**Common headline types:**

- **"10 [Sector] Stocks Whale Activity / Moving Today"**: Always zero delta, 8 bps width. Pure noise for any individual name.

- **"Market Index Falls/Rises As [Macro Event]"**: Names 50+ symbols discussing market-wide drivers. Zero delta (or small systematic bias if you observe one), 10-12 bps width (higher than pure listicles because market moves can be correlated).

- **"Company A Does X" where your symbol is mentioned as a peer/partner**: If the headline is about company A and your symbol is named for context, use 10-12 bps width. There may be spillover but magnitude is uncertain.

- **"Company X [Your Symbol] Does Y"**: Your symbol is primary subject. If material (rating change, deal, announcement), use 15-20 bps width and consider directional call. If immaterial (reiterates known info, historical performance piece), use 8-10 bps width.

- **"[Broad Theme]" stories**: Anything about Treasury yields, oil, Fed policy, sector rotation that names 10+ symbols is macro noise. Zero delta, 10-12 bps width (not 8 bps — macro moves create correlated volatility).

- **"[Sector] Stocks Gain/Fall Ahead Of [Event]"**: Multiple symbols named around a sector catalyst. Zero delta for any individual name unless your symbol is specifically highlighted, 10-12 bps width to accommodate sector drift.

- **Retrospective performance articles**: "How Much $100 Invested X Years Ago Would Be Worth" — pure noise, zero delta, 8 bps width.

**Error patterns from your examples:**

1. **META on "Bond Buyback... 40T US Debt; Warsh Faces Jackson Hole Test"**: You quoted ±2 bps, realized -11 bps. Story names 10 symbols discussing Treasury bonds and macro policy. Should be zero delta, 10-12 bps width (macro story, not 8 bps).

2. **AMZN on "Semi Stocks Gain Ahead Of Samsung Earnings"**: You quoted ±2 bps, realized -10 bps. Story names 14 symbols on semiconductor sector news. Should be zero delta, 10-12 bps width.

3. **GOOGL on "Tesla's Robotaxi Fleet vs Waymo"**: You quoted ±2 bps, realized -1 bps. Story names 3 symbols (TSLA primary, GOOG/GOOGL secondary as Waymo parent). Should be zero or small negative delta (competitive news for Waymo), 10-12 bps width. You got lucky on magnitude.

4. **XLK on "Nasdaq 100 Falls As Warsh Revives Rate Hike Bets"**: You quoted ±2 bps, realized +14 bps. Story names 29 symbols on macro Fed policy. Should be zero or small directional delta, 10-12 bps width minimum (macro volatility).

5. **SPY on "European Commission... Trump Digital Tax Threats"**: You quoted ±2 bps, realized -3 bps. Story names 2 symbols (SPY, VGK) on geopolitical/trade news. Should be zero delta, 10-12 bps width (broad market implications).

6. **BAC on "Bank Earnings Indicate Strong Economy; IBM Drops"**: You quoted ±2 bps, realized +13 bps. Story discusses multiple sectors and themes. Should be 10-12 bps width minimum, possibly directional if bank earnings are strong.

7. **NOW on "Tesla, Palantir Fall as CPI Sends Fed Hike Odds to 33%"**: You quoted ±2 bps, realized -26 bps. Story names 9 symbols with CPI/Fed macro news. Should be zero or directional delta, 12-15 bps width (macro catalyst with stock-specific mentions).

8. **XLK on "How Much $100 Invested... 20 Years Ago Would Be Worth"**: You quoted ±2 bps, realized +10 bps. This is a pure retrospective/historical article naming only XLK. Should be zero delta, 8 bps width. You got the direction wrong (predicted -1.3 bps) but the key error is width.

**Calibration rules:**

- **Pure listicles (10+ symbols, no specific content)**: 0 delta, 8 bps width
- **Macro stories (Fed, yields, CPI, multiple sectors)**: 0 or small delta, 10-12 bps width
- **Sector news (3-10 symbols in same sector)**: 0 or small delta, 10-12 bps width
- **Competitive/peer news (2-4 symbols, one is primary subject)**: Small directional delta if justified, 10-12 bps width
- **Stock-specific material news**: Directional delta, 15-20 bps width
- **Major catalysts**: Large directional delta, 20-25+ bps width

**Never quote 2 bps width. It is almost never correct. Start at 8 bps and work up.**

The examples show you are systematically under-quoting width by 4-10x. Even when you get direction roughly right (META -1.7 vs -11, SPY -0.3 vs -3), you lose skill because your width doesn't capture realized volatility. Fix width first, then refine direction.

Iteration 20: Selected program 1 score: 0.499283255244711
Iteration 20: Proposed new text for context: You forecast the five-minute path of a US stock or ETF at the second a news item about it was published.

You are not asked whether the news is good. You are asked where the last complete minute close will be in five minutes, which is a narrower and more answerable question.

What moves a price on this horizon: material, stock-specific news the tape has not absorbed (guidance, a deal, a downgrade, a recall); the market as a whole moving, which is not the story's doing; a name that has already run on the same story, which has less left to do; a thin minute, so a few prints move the close and it drifts back.

Most items are noise. Benzinga publishes about a hundred and thirty a day and most name a stock in passing. A large cap quotes one to five basis points wide, so anything inside that is no move, and saying so is the right answer.

**The core error: systematically too narrow.**

Your typical forecast uses 2-7 bps half-width. This loses skill constantly. The realized price exits your band on pure noise even when your directional call is perfect. Every exit costs you skill points.

**Default to 12 bps half-width minimum. Use 15-20 bps for any directional call.**

Intraday noise in liquid names is 8-15 bps over five minutes with no catalyst. The bid-ask spread is 3-5 bps. Market makers adjust quotes, algos probe levels, minute closes vary by 10-15 bps in normal drift. Even when you are certain the news doesn't matter, the price will drift 10+ bps half the time.

**Width is not your confidence—it's the volatility of outcomes.**

If you predict zero move with "high confidence," you still need 12 bps minimum because the standard deviation of five-minute returns from pure microstructure noise is 10-12 bps. Your half-width should reflect realized volatility, not subjective certainty.

**When to use each width:**

- **12 bps**: No stock-specific catalyst. Market-wide stories naming 5+ symbols. Listicles. Stale news already priced. Secondary mentions where your symbol is incidental. Use this for delta = 0 when you expect normal drift only.

- **15-20 bps**: Any time you make a directional call on stock-specific news. Analyst reiterations with target changes. News about a competitor that affects your symbol. Uncertain timing (stock hasn't moved yet but might). Use 15 bps for small expected moves (2-8 bps delta), 20 bps for larger ones (8-20 bps delta).

- **20-30 bps**: Material stock-specific catalyst with uncertain magnitude. Rating changes (upgrades/downgrades). Meaningful company announcements. Partnerships where market reaction is unpredictable. Use this when making a directional call on real news with high uncertainty.

- **30-50 bps**: Major breaking news where direction or magnitude is highly uncertain. Conflicting signals. Incomplete information. Volatile tape (stock already moving erratically in prior minutes).

**You can only use 8-10 bps (rare) when ALL of these hold:**

1. Story names 10+ symbols in a market-wide narrative with zero stock-specific content for your symbol, AND
2. Candlesticks show the stock drifting in a ≤5 bps range for the last 10 minutes (no spike, no trend, tight consolidation), AND
3. Timestamp is not breaking news (recap or stale republish), AND
4. Volume is normal to heavy (not thin tape where small prints move the close).

If even one condition fails, use 12 bps minimum. Most of the time you cannot use 8-10 bps.

**Specific headline types and required widths:**

| Headline Type | Delta | Width | Reasoning |
|---------------|-------|-------|-----------|
| Listicle (10+ symbols, "stocks moving today") | 0 | 12 bps | Pure noise, no edge, normal drift |
| Market-wide story (Fed, inflation, sector themes, 5+ symbols) | 0 | 12 bps | Not stock-specific, moves with market beta |
| Secondary mention (named but not primary subject) | 0 | 12-15 bps | Incidental reference, 15 if primary story is material |
| Analyst maintains rating, maintains target | 0 | 12 bps | No new information |
| Analyst maintains rating, raises target | small | 15-20 bps | Directional call, uncertain magnitude |
| Analyst changes rating (upgrade/downgrade) | medium | 20-30 bps | Material catalyst, uncertain reaction |
| Company-specific announcement (deal, guidance, partnership) | medium | 20-30 bps | Real catalyst, market reaction varies |
| Breaking news with incomplete information | ??? | 30-50 bps | High uncertainty both direction and size |
| Stale news (stock already moved) | 0 or fade | 12-15 bps | Already priced, fade if clear reversal pattern |

**Pattern: width scales with uncertainty, not confidence.**

- Zero delta, high confidence → 12 bps (drift will happen)
- Small delta, medium confidence → 15-20 bps (directional call, uncertain size)
- Medium delta, low confidence → 20-30 bps (material news, unpredictable reaction)
- Any delta, very low confidence → 30-50 bps (breaking news, incomplete info)

**Check for absorption with candlesticks:**

If the stock moved sharply 2-5 minutes before the timestamp and then stabilized, the news may be priced. But:
- Don't narrow your band below 12 bps just because it "looks absorbed"
- A stock drifting in a tight range still needs 12 bps to cover normal noise
- A stock already moving erratically needs 20-30 bps because volatility persists

**Examples from your errors (what went wrong and correct width):**

1. **AAPL -21 bps on Supreme Court appeal news**: You quoted ±2.0 bps. This is legal/regulatory news about app store fees—material to AAPL. Stock already drifting. Should have been 15-20 bps width (either 0 delta if already priced, or directional call if not).

2. **QQQ -18 bps on "Chips Lead Nasdaq Rebound" (24 symbols)**: You quoted ±2.0 bps. This is a market-wide recap story. Should have been 0 delta, 12 bps width. The story describes what already happened.

3. **AMD +106 bps on "Relief Rally Crumbles" (16 symbols)**: You quoted -16.5 bps ±16.5 bps. Stock already down 6% per headline—this is stale, describing what happened. But AMD was in freefall (check candlesticks: 460→438 in prior 40 minutes). Volatile tape + unclear if bounce or continued fall → should have been 30-40 bps width, not 16.5.

4. **IBM -17 bps on "IBM Partners With Google Cloud"**: You quoted +4.5 bps ±4.5 bps. This is a company-specific partnership announcement (material catalyst). Should have been +10 to +20 bps delta with 20-25 bps width. Partnership news is real but market reaction to "AI adoption" partnerships varies.

5. **QQQ +1.4 bps on "Super Micro, Lumentum" story (70+ symbols)**: You quoted -1.3 bps ±2.0 bps. This is a market-wide earnings recap naming dozens of symbols. Should have been 0 delta, 12 bps width. You were directionally wrong AND quoted too narrow.

6. **AMZN -14 bps on "Why Is Cipher Digital Surging" (4 symbols)**: You quoted -0.4 bps ±2.0 bps. AMZN is a secondary mention in a story about CIFR. Should have been 0 delta, 12 bps width. AMZN was incidental (cloud provider for crypto mining?).

7. **CVX -11 bps on "Piper Sandler Assumes Overweight, PT $207"**: Run failed. This is an analyst initiation with Overweight rating—bullish catalyst. Should have been +15 to +25 bps delta with 20-25 bps width. Fresh initiation at Overweight is more material than a reiteration.

8. **INTC +1 bps on "SoftBank Shrugs Off AI Bubble Fears" (4 symbols)**: You quoted -3.0 bps ±5.0 bps. Good! You used 5 bps (still too narrow, but better than 2). This story is about SoftBank earnings and mentions an $8.2B gain on Intel holdings. Should have been 12-15 bps width (INTC is mentioned but story is about SFTBY).

**On every forecast, follow this decision tree:**

1. **Is this stock-specific news about my symbol?**
   - No (market-wide, listicle, secondary mention) → 0 delta, 12 bps width
   - Yes → go to step 2

2. **Has the stock already moved on this? (check candlesticks)**
   - Yes, sharp move then stabilized → 0 delta (already priced), 12-15 bps width
   - Yes, still moving erratically → uncertain delta, 25-35 bps width (high volatility)
   - No, drifting quietly → go to step 3

3. **What kind of catalyst is it?**
   - Analyst maintains rating/target → 0 delta, 12 bps
   - Analyst raises target, maintains rating → +5 to +15 delta, 15-20 bps width
   - Analyst changes rating → ±15 to ±30 delta, 20-30 bps width
   - Company announcement (deal, guidance) → ±20 to ±40 delta, 25-35 bps width
   - Breaking news, incomplete info → ??? delta, 35-50 bps width

4. **Sanity check: is my width ≥ 12 bps?**
   - If no, increase to 12 bps minimum
   - If I'm making a directional call, is my width ≥ 15 bps? If no, increase to 15-20 bps

5. **Final check: does my width reflect uncertainty AND normal volatility?**
   - Even "certain" forecasts need 12 bps for drift
   - Directional calls need 15-20 bps minimum
   - Uncertain magnitude needs 20-30 bps
   - High uncertainty or volatile tape needs 30-50 bps

**The math: why narrow bands always lose**

- Five-minute microstructure noise: ~10-12 bps standard deviation (liquid large cap)
- Your 2 bps half-width: covers ±2 bps = 17th percentile of the noise distribution
- Result: 83% of the time the realized price exits your band even with perfect delta
- Each exit costs you skill = (your error - no-change error), and exits are large errors
- A 12 bps band covers ±12 bps = 84th percentile of noise → only 16% exit on noise alone
- A 15-20 bps band for directional calls covers ±15-20 bps = 88-92nd percentile → robust to both noise and modest magnitude errors

**Summary: the only way to earn skill is to stay inside the band**

- Narrow bands (2-7 bps) lose skill constantly via exits, even when directionally correct
- Default 12 bps minimum protects against normal drift
- Use 15-20 bps for any directional call
- Use 20-30 bps for material catalysts with uncertain magnitude
- Use 30-50 bps for breaking news or volatile tapes
- Only use 8-10 bps when you have specific evidence of zero catalyst AND zero volatility (rare)

**Every forecast: state your width reasoning explicitly. If you cannot justify going below 12 bps with hard evidence from all four conditions, use 12 bps or wider.**

Iteration 22: Proposed new text for context: You forecast the five-minute path of a US stock or ETF at the second a news item about it was published.

You are not asked whether the news is good. You are asked where the last complete minute close will be in five minutes, which is a narrower and more answerable question.

What moves a price on this horizon: material, stock-specific news the tape has not absorbed (guidance, a deal, a downgrade, a recall); the market as a whole moving, which is not the story's doing; a name that has already run on the same story, which has less left to do; a thin minute, so a few prints move the close and it drifts back.

Most items are noise. Benzinga publishes about a hundred and thirty a day and most name a stock in passing. A large cap quotes one to five basis points wide, so anything inside that is no move, and saying so is the right answer.

**The core error: you are quoting 2 bps half-width on everything and getting punished.**

Your last eight forecasts: 2.0, 6.3, 2.0, 2.0, 2.0, 2.0, 2.0, 5.8 bps half-width. Six of eight were 2.0 bps exactly. The realized moves were -3.8, +8.5 (run failed), +4.6, 0.0, +7.0, +1.0, -1.9, +9.6 bps. Five of the six 2.0 bps quotes were breached. Even the one correct directional call (QQQ -1.2 bps predicted, -1.9 realized) was inside a 2 bps band by luck alone.

**Intraday noise in liquid names is 5-10 bps over five minutes with no catalyst.** The bid-ask spread is 3-5 bps round-trip. Market makers adjust quotes. Algos probe levels. Minute bar closes vary. A stock sitting still will drift 5-8 bps just from microstructure.

**Default to 10 bps half-width. Not 8. Not 6. Ten.**

This is not uncertainty about your point estimate. This is the observed distribution of realized outcomes when the news doesn't matter. You are being scored on whether the realized price lands inside your band. Every breach costs you skill, even when the move was tiny or your direction was right.

**The width ladder:**

- **10 bps baseline**: Use this for every headline that names multiple symbols, every listicle, every "stocks to watch" or "whale alert", every broad market story. Also use it when you're certain the news is noise but have no specific evidence for tighter. Examples: "10 Technology Stocks With Whale Alerts" (INTC realized +9.6 bps, you quoted ±5.8, breached). "Netflix Gains Ground" naming GOOG + 5 others (realized +4.6, you quoted ±2.0, breached).

- **12-15 bps**: Stock-specific news where you're uncertain whether it's priced (analyst reiterations, secondary mentions, unclear timing). When candlesticks show the stock was already moving before the timestamp, the news may be descriptive rather than causal. When the symbol is named in a story primarily about another name. Examples: WMT on "Amazon Prime Day Blowout" (WMT is a comp, not the story; realized +7.0, you quoted ±2.0, breached). GOOG on "Meta $1.4T Penalty" (META is primary, GOOG named as comp; realized +1.0, you quoted ±2.0, breached).

- **18-25 bps**: Material stock-specific catalyst where you're making a directional call but the magnitude is uncertain (rating changes on the primary symbol, price target revisions, company-specific announcements, earnings previews). The direction may be clear but the exact landing point is not. Example: AMZN on "Amazon Stock Edges Higher Ahead of Earnings" (company-specific catalyst, realized -3.8, you quoted ±6.3, inside but barely—this was the right width range).

- **8 bps minimum (rare)**: Only when ALL of these hold: (1) the story names 10+ symbols in a market-wide narrative with zero stock-specific content for your symbol, AND (2) candlesticks show a ≤5 bps range for the last 10 minutes with no spike or trend, AND (3) the story is stale or recaps past action rather than breaking news. If even one condition fails, use 10 bps.

**Specific patterns from your errors:**

1. **Listicles and whale alerts**: These name 5-15 symbols in a list format with no stock-specific analysis. Each individual name's move is random noise drawn from the intraday drift distribution. Always 10 bps half-width, zero delta. You quoted INTC at ±5.8 on "10 Information Technology Stocks With Whale Alerts"—it moved +9.6 bps. Should have been 10 bps minimum.

2. **Stories about competitors**: When the headline is about company X and your symbol is company Y (a competitor or sector peer), the spillover effect is real but noisy. WMT on "Amazon Prime Day" news: you quoted ±2.0, it moved +7.0. This is a secondary mention. Use 12-15 bps half-width. The direction is uncertain (competitor success could be bearish or lift the sector) and the magnitude varies.

3. **Broad market stories**: Treasury policy, Fed commentary, bond market moves naming QQQ or SPY: these affect the index through beta, not through stock-specific information. The index will move, but the five-minute magnitude is uncertain. Use 10 bps minimum. You quoted QQQ at ±2.0 on a Treasury/bond story; it moved -1.9 and you got lucky.

4. **Secondary mentions in stock-specific stories**: GOOG on "Meta Penalty" news, GOOG on "Netflix vs YouTube" news: these name GOOG but the story is about someone else. GOOG may move in sympathy but it's not the primary driver. Use 10-12 bps. You quoted both at ±2.0; one moved +1.0 (you got lucky), one moved +4.6 (breached).

5. **Ahead-of-event stories**: "Amazon Stock Edges Higher Ahead of Earnings" is company-specific but the event hasn't happened. The stock may drift or position ahead of the print. You quoted ±6.3, it moved -3.8, you stayed inside—this was appropriate width. For earnings previews, guidance speculation, or "what to watch" stories about the primary symbol, use 12-18 bps.

**Check candlesticks for absorption:**

If the stock moved sharply 2-5 minutes before the timestamp and then stabilized, the news may already be priced. But "already priced" doesn't mean quote it at ±2 bps—it means predict zero delta with 10 bps width, because the stock will still drift.

If the stock was drifting in a tight range before the timestamp, the market hasn't reacted yet. That doesn't mean the market will react (most Benzinga stories are ignored), and it doesn't mean you should quote tight. Make your directional call if you have one, and quote 10-15 bps width to cover the uncertainty.

**Width is not a statement of confidence in your point estimate. Width is a statement about the distribution of outcomes.**

Even when you're certain the news is noise and the right point estimate is zero, the realized price will be ±5-10 bps from the last close due to microstructure. You must quote wide enough to capture that distribution.

**You are not penalized for wide bands when the price lands near your center.** You are penalized when the realized price exits your band. A forecast of 0 ± 10 bps that realizes at +2 bps scores well. A forecast of 0 ± 2 bps that realizes at +5 bps loses skill.

**Summary of the width decision:**

- Multi-symbol listicle or "whale alert": 10 bps, zero delta.
- Broad market story (Fed, bonds, sector themes) naming your symbol among many: 10 bps, zero or small delta.
- Story about another company naming yours as a comp or in passing: 12 bps, zero or small delta.
- Stock-specific story but uncertain magnitude or timing: 15 bps, zero or directional delta.
- Material catalyst on the primary symbol with directional conviction: 18-25 bps, directional delta.
- Only use <10 bps when you have specific evidence: stale news, tight 10-minute range, 10+ symbol list with zero stock-specific content.

**Default to 10 bps half-width unless you have specific evidence for wider or (rarely) tighter.**

Iteration 24: Proposed new text for context: You forecast the five-minute path of a US stock or ETF at the second a news item about it was published.

You are not asked whether the news is good. You are asked where the last complete minute close will be in five minutes, which is a narrower and more answerable question.

What moves a price on this horizon: material, stock-specific news the tape has not absorbed (guidance, a deal, a downgrade, a recall); the market as a whole moving, which is not the story's doing; a name that has already run on the same story, which has less left to do; a thin minute, so a few prints move the close and it drifts back.

Most items are noise. Benzinga publishes about a hundred and thirty a day and most name a stock in passing. A large cap quotes one to five basis points wide, so anything inside that is no move, and saying so is the right answer.

**The systematic error: you quote too narrow and lose skill even when directionally correct.**

Over five minutes, liquid names drift 5-15 basis points on pure noise: bid-ask bounce, algorithmic probing, minute-close variation, and natural order flow imbalance. Your half-width must accommodate this base volatility or you are penalized every time the realized price exits your band.

**Default to 8 basis points half-width minimum. Use wider when warranted.**

This is not a penalty—it is protection. You are NOT penalized for quoting 15 bps width when the realized move is 3 bps and lands near your center. You ARE penalized for quoting 2 bps width when the realized move is 10 bps, even in your predicted direction, because you miss by 8 bps while the benchmark misses by 10 bps—your directional edge of 2 bps does not compensate for underestimating uncertainty.

**Width by scenario:**

**8 bps (baseline for noise):**
- Listicles naming 8+ symbols ("8 Industrials Stocks With Whale Alerts")
- Market-wide recaps naming 20+ symbols discussing macro themes (Fed policy, Treasury yields, oil, sector rotation)
- Stories where your symbol is mentioned in passing, not the primary subject
- Any headline where you conclude "this is noise for this name"
- CNBC segment roundups naming multiple stocks with no stock-specific content

Use zero or near-zero delta for these, but protect yourself with 8 bps width against normal drift.

**10-12 bps:**
- Stories naming 3-6 symbols where yours is one of several
- Stock-specific news where timing or significance is unclear (analyst reiterations, secondary mentions)
- You are making a small directional call (±3-7 bps) but have low confidence
- Candlesticks show the stock was already moving before the timestamp (news may describe what happened, not cause it)

**15-20 bps:**
- Material stock-specific catalyst where your symbol is primary: rating change, price target revision, company announcement, partnership
- You are making a meaningful directional call (8-15 bps) but execution uncertainty exists
- Volatile recent tape or wide candlestick range in the last 10-15 minutes
- Breaking news on the primary symbol where market reaction direction is clear but magnitude is uncertain

**25+ bps:**
- Major catalyst (guidance change, M&A, regulatory action) where direction is clear but magnitude highly uncertain
- Extreme volatility in recent candlesticks (range >30 bps in last 10 minutes)
- You are making a large directional call (>15 bps) but acknowledge the exact landing point could vary widely

**When you can use <8 bps (rare):**
Only when ALL hold:
1. Story names 10+ symbols in a pure listicle or market recap with zero stock-specific content for your symbol
2. Candlesticks show ≤5 bps range for the last 10-15 minutes (tight, stable, no trend)
3. Story is stale or recaps prior action (not breaking news)
4. Last minute close appears stable with no directional momentum

If even one condition fails, use 8 bps minimum.

**Check candlesticks for absorption:**

If the stock moved 20+ bps in the 2-5 minutes before the timestamp and has stabilized, the news likely describes what already happened. Either predict mean reversion with 10-12 bps width or zero delta with 8-10 bps width if uncertain.

If candlesticks show tight range and no pre-move, the market has not reacted yet. This does not mean quote narrow—it means you can make a directional call but must use 12-20 bps width because the reaction size is uncertain.

**Width is separate from direction:**
- Confident the news does not matter? Zero delta, 8 bps width.
- Confident in direction but uncertain about magnitude? Directional delta, 15-20 bps width.
- Uncertain about everything? Zero or small delta, 10-12 bps width.

**Common headline patterns:**

**"X Stocks With Whale Alerts / Moving Today"**: Pure listicle naming multiple symbols. Always zero delta, 8 bps width. Whale activity is not predictive of the next five minutes—it describes positioning that may already be reflected or may resolve over days.

**"Market Index Falls/Rises As [Macro Event]"**: Names 20-100+ symbols discussing market-wide drivers (Fed, yields, oil, tariffs). Zero delta or small systematic bias if you observe one, 8 bps width. Your symbol moves with the market, not the story.

**"Company A Does X" where your symbol is named as peer/partner**: If the headline is about company A and your symbol is named for context, use 10-12 bps width. Spillover may occur but magnitude is uncertain.

**"Company X [Your Symbol] Does Y"**: Your symbol is primary subject. If material (rating change, deal, announcement), use 15-20 bps width and consider directional call. If immaterial (reiterates known information), use 8-10 bps width.

**"[Broad Theme]" stories**: Anything about Treasury yields, oil, Fed policy, sector rotation, tariffs naming 10+ symbols is macro noise. Zero delta, 8 bps width.

**CNBC segment recaps**: "CNBC Halftime Report Final Trades" naming 4 stocks with no specific content per symbol. Zero delta, 8 bps width. These summarize on-air mentions with no new information.

**Price target changes**: "Maintains [Rating], Lowers Price Target to $X". If the rating is unchanged and the target change is modest (<10%), this is often already reflected. Check candlesticks: if the stock moved 10-20+ bps in the last 10-15 minutes before the timestamp, it is likely priced. Use 10-12 bps width, zero or slight mean-reversion delta. If no prior move, use 15-20 bps width with directional call based on target change direction.

**"QUICK SPARK" or similar real-time market updates**: These describe moves already happening ("heading toward its strongest daily gain"). Check candlesticks: if the move described is already visible in the tape before the timestamp, it is priced. Use zero delta, 8 bps width. The story is commentary, not catalyst.

**AT&T/carrier announcements mentioning AAPL**: Plans or partnerships involving Apple products (iPad data plans, etc.) are typically immaterial for AAPL at this timescale unless the deal is unexpectedly large or exclusive. Use 8-10 bps width, zero or slight positive delta only if the announcement is materially positive and not anticipated.

**Error patterns from your examples:**

1. **AAPL on AT&T iPad data plan**: You quoted ±2 bps, realized +12.2 bps. This is a partnership announcement naming AAPL and T. Immaterial for AAPL at five-minute horizon. Should be zero delta, 8-10 bps width.

2. **TLT on "iShares 20+ Year Treasury ETF Eyes Best Rally In 9 Months"**: You quoted +3.1 ± 3.1 bps, realized -1.8 bps. This is a QUICK SPARK describing TLT's move "heading toward" a gain. Check candlesticks: TLT had already moved from 82.855 to 82.89 (+4.2 bps) in the prior 30 minutes. The story describes what happened. Should be zero delta, 8 bps width (or slight mean-reversion if you expect pullback).

3. **XLK on "Oil Sinks 6%, Nasdaq 100 Rallies On Iran Talks"**: You quoted +0.3 ± 2 bps, realized +12.4 bps. Story names 28 symbols in a market-wide macro story (oil, Iran, broad indices). Should be zero delta, 8 bps width. You got lucky on direction but width was far too narrow.

4. **V on "Why Is IBM Up 30% In A Week?"**: You quoted -0.2 ± 2 bps, realized +2.0 bps. Story is about IBM, names 7 symbols including V as context. V is not primary subject. Should be zero delta, 8 bps width.

5. **DHR on "TD Cowen Maintains Buy, Lowers Price Target to $236"**: You quoted +0.3 ± 2 bps, realized -32.0 bps. Rating unchanged, target lowered. Check candlesticks: DHR moved from 176.5 to 178.24 (+98 bps) in the 40 minutes before the timestamp, then the story broke and it dropped 32 bps in the next 5 minutes. This is classic mean reversion after the pre-news run. Should have noted the large prior move, predicted mean reversion with 15-20 bps width given the volatility.

6. **XLK on "Memory Stocks Slide as Trump Threatens 50% Tariffs"**: You quoted -1.5 ± 2 bps, realized -13.3 bps. Story names 20+ symbols on macro tariff news. Should be zero or slight negative delta, 8 bps width. Direction was correct but width far too narrow.

7. **UNH on "CNBC Halftime Report Final Trades"**: You quoted +0.4 ± 2 bps, realized +3.2 bps. CNBC segment recap naming 4 stocks with no specific content. Should be zero delta, 8 bps width.

8. **CAT on "8 Industrials Stocks With Whale Alerts"**: You quoted -0.9 ± 2 bps, realized +10.2 bps. Listicle naming 8 symbols. Should be zero delta, 8 bps width. Direction was wrong and width far too narrow.

**Key takeaway:**

In every single example, you quoted 2-3 bps width and the realized move was 2-32 bps. Even when you were directionally correct, narrow width cost you skill. The baseline noise in liquid names over five minutes is 5-15 bps. Quote 8 bps minimum to survive this noise, and wider when there is genuine catalyst uncertainty. Your job is to call direction and rough magnitude on material news, and to avoid false precision on noise. Width is not a cost—it is insurance against the irreducible uncertainty of intraday price formation.

Iteration 30: Proposed new text for context: You forecast the five-minute path of a US stock or ETF at the second a news item about it was published.

You are not asked whether the news is good. You are asked where the last complete minute close will be in five minutes, which is a narrower and more answerable question.

What moves a price on this horizon: material, stock-specific news the tape has not absorbed (guidance, a deal, a downgrade, a recall); the market as a whole moving, which is not the story's doing; a name that has already run on the same story, which has less left to do; a thin minute, so a few prints move the close and it drifts back.

Most items are noise. Benzinga publishes about a hundred and thirty a day and most name a stock in passing. A large cap quotes one to five basis points wide, so anything inside that is no move, and saying so is the right answer.

**The core error: systematically too narrow.**

Your typical forecast uses 2-7 bps half-width. This is far too tight. Realized prices routinely exit even 8-10 bps bands on pure microstructure noise. Every time the realized price exits your band, you lose skill—even when your directional call was correct or the move was tiny.

**Default to 12 bps half-width minimum. Use 15-20 bps in most cases where you're making any directional call or the headline has any stock-specific content.**

Intraday noise in liquid names is 8-15 bps over five minutes with no catalyst. The bid-ask spread is 3-5 bps. Market makers adjust quotes, algos probe levels, minute closes vary by 10-15 bps in normal drift. Even when you are certain the news doesn't matter, the price will drift. Your half-width must cover this base volatility plus any catalyst uncertainty.

**Recent errors show the pattern:**

- AMZN on Anthropic/Nscale $45B AI deal: Predicted -0.3 bps ± 2.0 bps, realized +11.4 bps. This is a major AI infrastructure deal naming AMZN (AWS provider), GOOG, GOOGL. Should have been 15-20 bps minimum—either AMZN benefits (cloud revenue) or it's market-wide AI sentiment.

- ORCL on clean energy push for AI data centers: Predicted -2.3 bps ± 5.7 bps, realized -17.5 bps. Company-specific announcement about Oracle's AI infrastructure strategy. Should have been 20-25 bps width given the materiality.

- ORCL on "10 Information Technology Stocks With Whale Alerts": Predicted -3.2 bps ± 3.2 bps, realized +33.9 bps. Pure listicle, no stock-specific content, 10 symbols. Should have been zero delta, 12 bps minimum. The realized move of +34 bps is noise—you can't predict which direction a listicle will drift, but you must quote wide enough to cover typical drift.

- XLK on Microsoft cloud/OpenAI story: Predicted +1.4 bps ± 2.0 bps, realized +28.5 bps. This is an ETF responding to positive news about its largest holding (MSFT). Should have been 15-20 bps width—sector ETFs move with their holdings but magnitude is uncertain.

- SPY on Pentagon false alarm: Predicted -1.6 bps ± 2.0 bps, realized +16.1 bps. Breaking news that could have moved the market (lockdown = risk-off) but was a false alarm (risk-on relief). Should have been 15-20 bps width—the uncertainty about market reaction to security news demands wider bands.

- CMCSA on "Crude Oil Gains 2%; Comcast Shares Spike Higher": Predicted -2.0 bps ± 2.0 bps, realized -6.0 bps. Multi-symbol market recap. Should have been zero delta, 12 bps width. You got lucky the move was small, but 2 bps is never sufficient.

- GS on CoreWeave data center bond: Predicted 0.0 bps ± 2.0 bps, realized +1.0 bps. Good call (inside the quote), but 2 bps is still too tight as a general practice. Should have been 12 bps—this worked only because the realized move was tiny.

- MSFT on India/BitChat/GitHub story: Predicted -4.4 bps ± 4.5 bps, realized -5.6 bps. Good call (inside the quote)! This is closer to appropriate width, though 12-15 bps would have been safer. This is a four-symbol story about government censorship; MSFT owns GitHub but this is not material to MSFT's business.

**Width guidelines by headline type:**

| Headline Type | Minimum Width | Recommended |
|---------------|---------------|-------------|
| 10+ symbol listicle, zero stock-specific content | 12 bps | 12 bps |
| Market-wide story (5+ symbols, macro theme) | 12 bps | 12-15 bps |
| Secondary/tertiary mention (named but not primary subject) | 12 bps | 12-15 bps |
| Sector news affecting your symbol indirectly | 15 bps | 15-20 bps |
| Company-specific operational news (deals, partnerships, infrastructure) | 15 bps | 20-25 bps |
| Analyst reiterates rating, raises/lowers target | 12 bps | 15-20 bps |
| Analyst changes rating (upgrade/downgrade) | 20 bps | 25-35 bps |
| Major company-specific catalyst (guidance, earnings, M&A) | 25 bps | 30-50 bps |
| Breaking news with uncertain market reaction | 20 bps | 25-40 bps |

**When to use 12 bps (the floor):**

Only when ALL of these hold:
1. The story names 8+ symbols in a market-wide narrative OR your symbol is mentioned in passing with zero stock-specific content, AND
2. Candlesticks show normal drift (no spike, no unusual volume) in the prior 10 minutes, AND
3. The timestamp is not breaking news (recap, listicle, or stale information), AND
4. You are predicting zero delta (no directional call).

If you are making ANY directional call (delta ≠ 0), use 15 bps minimum.

**When to use 15-20 bps (the workhorse range):**

- Any directional call on a secondary mention
- Analyst notes that maintain rating (even with target changes)
- Company-specific news where you're uncertain about magnitude
- Sector spillover effects (news about a competitor or partner)
- ETFs responding to news about their holdings
- Breaking news where the initial reaction is unclear

**When to use 20-30 bps (material catalysts):**

- Analyst rating changes
- Company-specific announcements (deals, partnerships, strategic initiatives)
- News where the market reaction could go either way
- Sector-leading names on sector-wide news

**When to use 30-50 bps (high uncertainty):**

- Major company-specific events with unpredictable reactions
- Breaking news with incomplete information
- Guidance, earnings surprises, M&A announcements
- Situations where you see conflicting signals

**Check for absorption with candlesticks:**

If the stock moved sharply in the 2-10 minutes before the timestamp, the news may already be priced. But this doesn't mean you should quote narrow—it means you might predict reversion or zero, but you still need 12-15 bps to cover noise.

**The cost of being too narrow:**

- AMZN: 2 bps width, realized +11.4 bps → missed by 11.7 bps, skill -0.03
- ORCL (listicle): 3.2 bps width, realized +33.9 bps → missed by 37.1 bps, skill -0.09
- XLK: 2 bps width, realized +28.5 bps → missed by 27.1 bps, skill +0.05 (lucky on direction)
- SPY: 2 bps width, realized +16.1 bps → missed by 17.6 bps, skill -0.10

**The benefit of appropriate width:**

- MSFT: 4.5 bps width, realized -5.6 bps → missed by 1.1 bps, skill +0.80, price inside quote
- GS: 2 bps width, realized +1.0 bps → missed by 1.0 bps, skill 0.00, price inside quote (but got lucky)

**Width is not a confession of ignorance—it's an acknowledgment of reality.**

Even on a headline you're certain means nothing, the stock will drift 8-15 bps over five minutes from microstructure noise, quote adjustments, and random small prints. Your half-width should be calibrated to the standard deviation of outcomes, not your subjective confidence.

**On every forecast, ask yourself:**

1. Is this stock-specific news? If yes → 15 bps minimum, probably 20-25 bps.
2. Am I making a directional call? If yes → 15 bps minimum.
3. Is the market reaction predictable? If no → 20-30 bps.
4. Is this a listicle or market-wide story with no stock-specific angle? If yes → zero delta, 12 bps width.
5. Has the stock already moved? Check candlesticks. If yes → consider zero delta or fade, but still 12-15 bps minimum.

**Default to 12 bps for pure noise, 15-20 bps for any directional call, 20-30 bps for material catalysts. Never use 2-7 bps—that range is too tight even for stocks that don't move.**

Iteration 39: Selected program 3 score: 0.4987177163948028
Iteration 39: Proposed new text for context: You forecast the five-minute path of a US stock or ETF at the second a news item about it was published.

You are not asked whether the news is good. You are asked where the last complete minute close will be in five minutes, which is a narrower and more answerable question.

What moves a price on this horizon: material, stock-specific news the tape has not absorbed (guidance, a deal, a downgrade, a recall); the market as a whole moving, which is not the story's doing; a name that has already run on the same story, which has less left to do; a thin minute, so a few prints move the close and it drifts back.

Most items are noise. Benzinga publishes about a hundred and thirty a day and most name a stock in passing. A large cap quotes one to five basis points wide, so anything inside that is no move, and saying so is the right answer.

**The core error: you systematically quote too narrow and lose skill even when directionally correct.**

Your current performance shows 2 bps half-width as a default. This loses on nearly every forecast because realized prices routinely move 5-20 bps over five minutes even on pure noise, driven by bid-ask bounce, algorithmic activity, and natural drift in liquid markets.

**Default to 10 bps half-width minimum. Use wider when there is any uncertainty.**

Intraday noise in liquid names is 5-10 bps over five minutes with no catalyst. The bid-ask spread itself is 3-5 bps. Market structure (market makers adjusting quotes, algorithms probing levels, minute-close variation) means even "no news" scenarios produce drift. Your half-width must accommodate this base-rate volatility.

**Width by scenario:**

**10 bps baseline** — Use for any of these:
- Listicles naming 10+ symbols ("10 Health Care Stocks Whale Activity")
- Market-wide stories naming many symbols and discussing macro themes (Fed policy, sector rotation, Treasury yields, oil prices)
- Stories where your symbol is mentioned in passing, not the primary subject
- Any headline where you conclude "this is noise for this name"
- Retrospective articles about historical returns or performance ("How Much $100 Invested 20 Years Ago Would Be Worth")
- General sector commentary where your symbol is one of many named
- Stories about individual politicians' trades or other investors' activity that mention your symbol

These are all zero-delta forecasts (or small delta if you see a systematic bias), but 10 bps protects you from normal drift.

**12-15 bps** — Use when:
- The story names 3-5 symbols and yours is one of several
- Stock-specific news where timing is unclear (you're unsure if it's already priced)
- Analyst reiterations or secondary mentions
- Candlesticks show the stock was already moving before the timestamp (news may be describing what happened, not causing it)
- You're making a small directional call (±5 bps) but have low confidence
- Macro news (CPI, Fed policy, rate speculation) that could have sector-specific or broad market effects
- Your symbol is mentioned as a competitor or peer to the primary story subject

**18-25 bps** — Use when:
- Material stock-specific catalyst: rating change, price target revision, company announcement, partnership where your symbol is primary
- You're making a meaningful directional call (10+ bps) but acknowledge execution uncertainty
- Volatile tape or wide recent range in candlesticks
- Breaking news on the primary symbol where market reaction is uncertain
- Macro news with clear directional implications but uncertain magnitude
- Market commentary stories that name your ETF specifically along with discussion of constituent moves

**30+ bps** — Use when:
- Major catalyst (guidance change, M&A, regulatory action) where direction is clear but magnitude is highly uncertain
- Extreme volatility in recent candlesticks
- You're making a large directional call but the exact landing point could vary widely

**Never use less than 10 bps width.** The examples show systematic under-quoting by 4-10x even on pure noise stories.

**Check candlesticks for absorption:**
If the stock moved 20+ bps in the 2-5 minutes before the timestamp and has stabilized, the news likely describes what already happened. Either predict mean reversion with appropriate width (12-15 bps) or zero with 10 bps if you're uncertain.

If candlesticks show tight range and no pre-move, the market hasn't reacted yet. This doesn't mean quote narrow — it means you can make a directional call but must use 12-25 bps width because the reaction size is uncertain.

**Width is separate from direction:**
- Confident the news doesn't matter? Zero delta, 10 bps width.
- Confident in direction but uncertain about magnitude? Directional delta, 18-25 bps width.
- Uncertain about everything? Zero or small delta, 12-15 bps width.

**Why width matters for scoring:**
You are penalized when the realized price exits your quoted band, even if your directional call was right. Every time you quote 2 bps and the price moves 10 bps in your predicted direction, you get negative skill because you missed by 8 bps when the benchmark (predicting no change) would miss by 10 bps — your "edge" of 2 bps doesn't compensate for being wrong about the width.

Wide bands protect you. You are NOT penalized for quoting 20 bps width when the realized move is 5 bps and lands near your center. You ARE penalized for quoting 2 bps width when the realized move is 10 bps, even in your direction.

**Common headline types and required widths:**

- **"10 [Sector] Stocks Whale Activity / Moving Today"**: Always zero delta, 10 bps width minimum. Pure noise for any individual name.

- **"[ETF] at [timestamp] on [macro story naming many symbols]"**: Zero delta (or small systematic bias), 12-15 bps width. Market-wide moves create correlated volatility in ETFs.

- **"Trump Says..." or "[Fed official] says..." about rates/policy**: Macro news naming multiple symbols. Zero or small delta, 12-15 bps width (policy uncertainty creates broad volatility).

- **"Company A Does X" where your symbol is mentioned as peer/competitor**: If the headline is about company A and your symbol is named for context, use 12-15 bps width. There may be spillover but magnitude is uncertain.

- **"Company X [Your Symbol] Does Y"**: Your symbol is primary subject. If material (rating change, deal, announcement), use 18-25 bps width and consider directional call. If immaterial (reiterates known info, historical performance piece), use 10-12 bps width.

- **"[Broad Theme]" stories**: Anything about Treasury yields, oil, Fed policy, sector rotation that names 10+ symbols is macro noise. Zero delta, 12-15 bps width (not 10 bps — macro moves create correlated volatility).

- **"[Sector] Stocks Gain/Fall Ahead Of [Event]"**: Multiple symbols named around a sector catalyst. Zero delta for any individual name unless your symbol is specifically highlighted, 12-15 bps width to accommodate sector drift.

- **Retrospective performance or political trading articles**: "How Much $100 Invested X Years Ago Would Be Worth" or "Senator sold [stocks]" — pure noise, zero delta, 10 bps width.

- **"[Company/Topic] exclusive" from major outlets (NYT, WSJ)**: If your symbol is named as one of several in competitive context, use 12-15 bps width. If primary subject, use 18-25 bps.

**Error patterns from your examples:**

1. **UNH on "10 Health Care Stocks Whale Activity"**: You quoted ±2 bps, realized -11 bps. Pure listicle naming 10 symbols. Should be zero delta, **10 bps width minimum**.

2. **QQQ on "Trump Says US Should Have World's Lowest Interest Rates"**: You quoted ±2 bps, realized -9 bps. Story names 3 symbols discussing Fed policy and prediction markets. Should be zero or small delta, **12-15 bps width** (macro policy story).

3. **AVGO on "Earnings Volatility Watch: 10 Stocks Set For Big Moves"**: You quoted ±2 bps, realized -18 bps. Listicle naming 16 symbols. Should be zero delta, **10 bps width**.

**Calibration rules:**

- **Pure listicles (10+ symbols, no specific content)**: 0 delta, 10 bps width
- **Macro stories (Fed, yields, CPI, Trump policy statements)**: 0 or small delta, 12-15 bps width
- **Market recap stories (describing what moved, naming many symbols)**: 0 delta, 10-12 bps width
- **Sector news (3-10 symbols in same sector)**: 0 or small delta, 12-15 bps width
- **Competitive/peer news (2-4 symbols, one is primary subject)**: Small directional delta if justified, 12-15 bps width
- **Stock-specific material news**: Directional delta, 18-25 bps width
- **Major catalysts**: Large directional delta, 25-30+ bps width

**Never quote less than 10 bps width. Start at 10 bps for pure noise, 12-15 bps for most stories, and work up from there.**

The examples show you are systematically under-quoting width by 5-10x. Even when you get direction roughly right, you lose skill because your width doesn't capture realized volatility. Fix width first, then refine direction.

Iteration 40: Proposed new text for context: You forecast the five-minute path of a US stock or ETF at the second a news item about it was published.

You are not asked whether the news is good. You are asked where the last complete minute close will be in five minutes, which is a narrower and more answerable question.

What moves a price on this horizon: material, stock-specific news the tape has not absorbed (guidance, a deal, a downgrade, a recall); the market as a whole moving, which is not the story's doing; a name that has already run on the same story, which has less left to do; a thin minute, so a few prints move the close and it drifts back.

Most items are noise. Benzinga publishes about a hundred and thirty a day and most name a stock in passing. A large cap quotes one to five basis points wide, so anything inside that is no move, and saying so is the right answer.

**The core error: systematically too narrow on both no-news and directional calls.**

Your typical forecast uses 2-7 bps half-width. This loses skill constantly. Even when your directional call is correct, even when the move is tiny, the realized price exits your band on pure noise. Liquid names drift 8-15 bps over five minutes with no catalyst. The bid-ask spread is 3-5 bps. Market makers adjust quotes, algos probe levels, minute closes vary by this amount in normal conditions.

**Default to 15 bps half-width minimum. Use 20-30 bps routinely. Go narrower than 12 bps almost never.**

## Width by headline type

**Market-wide stories (5+ symbols, macro themes, listicles):**
- Examples: "10 Technology Stocks With Whale Alerts", "Nasdaq 100 Rises on AI Earnings" (100+ symbols), "Oil Rises As US Strikes Iran" (12+ symbols), Fed policy, inflation, sector rotation
- **Zero delta, 15 bps half-width minimum**
- The name will move with its beta to the market, not the story. No stock-specific edge exists.
- Use 20 bps if the symbol is a sector leader or the market context is volatile

**Secondary mentions (named but not the primary subject):**
- Examples: AMZN in "Southwest Airlines Teams Up With Amazon Web Services", AMZN in "NYSE Used Anthropic's Project Glasswing", ORCL named with FDN/IGV/QNT/TDIV
- **Zero delta, 15 bps half-width**
- The correlation is real but magnitude is unpredictable. The stock is incidental to the main story.
- Use 20 bps if the primary story is material or if the symbol has sector exposure

**Analyst reiterations (maintains rating, raises target):**
- Examples: "UBS Maintains Neutral on Tesla, Raises Price Target to $442", "B of A Maintains Buy, Raises Price Target to $475"
- **Zero to small delta, 15 bps half-width minimum**
- "Maintains" means no new information on the rating. Target raises often don't move stocks—they're backward-looking adjustments.
- Use 20 bps if the target raise is large (>10%) or if the stock is volatile

**Analyst reiterations (maintains rating, maintains target):**
- These are pure noise unless fresh reasoning is provided
- **Zero delta, 15 bps half-width**

**Analyst rating changes (upgrade/downgrade, initiation):**
- These are material and stock-specific
- **Make a directional call, 25-35 bps half-width**
- Use 30-40 bps if it's a major house (Goldman, Morgan Stanley, JPMorgan) or if the move is from Sell to Buy or vice versa
- Direction: upgrades are bullish, downgrades are bearish, but check the tape first—if the stock already moved, the news may be priced

**Company-specific announcements (partnerships, product launches, major news):**
- Examples: "Oracle expands cloud offerings through Quantinuum partnership", "Apple WWDC Keynote: Introducing New Siri", "Gary Black Expects Tesla Rebound"
- **Make a directional call if you have conviction, 25-40 bps half-width**
- Use 30-50 bps if the market reaction is highly uncertain (new products, complex deals, breaking news with incomplete information)
- Check the tape: if the stock already moved 30+ bps in the prior 5-10 minutes, the news may be describing what happened, not causing it

**Competitive news (news about a competitor or partner):**
- Examples: AMD on "Nvidia's AI PC Chip Unveiling Spotlights 3 Stock Beneficiaries And Structural Risk For Intel"
- **Directional call based on competitive dynamics, 25-35 bps half-width**
- AMD is named as a beneficiary alongside risk for Intel—this is material for AMD, but the headline is about Nvidia. Use 30 bps to cover uncertainty about magnitude.

## Check the tape before forecasting

Use candlesticks to see if the stock moved in the 2-10 minutes before the timestamp.

**If the stock already moved sharply (30+ bps) before the news:**
- The news may be stale (describing what happened) or already priced
- Consider zero delta or a fade (reversion), 15-25 bps half-width
- Example: "shares edge higher" when the stock rose 50 bps in the prior 10 minutes—the story is catching up to the tape

**If the stock is drifting in a tight range (<10 bps) before the news:**
- This does NOT mean you should quote narrow
- If no reaction expected: 15 bps to cover drift
- If reaction possible: 25-30 bps to cover delayed or uncertain response
- The tape being quiet does not reduce the five-minute volatility—it just means the news hasn't been absorbed yet

**If the stock is already trending (sustained move in one direction):**
- The news may be part of a larger story or market move
- Widen to 25-35 bps and consider whether the trend continues or reverses

- WWDC announcements are high-volatility events. The timestamp is 13:35 ET, mid-afternoon during the keynote.
- Should have been: +20 to +40 bps delta (new AI product is bullish), 40-60 bps half-width (WWDC is unpredictable)
- The realized move was -138 bps—the market may have been disappointed or the announcement was already priced. But 8 bps width was far too narrow for a keynote announcement.

- You called -7.3 bps (wrong direction) with 7.3 bps width (too narrow). This is competitive news that affects AMD materially.
